### TL;DR

Proposes a noise-robust method to test genuine multipartite nonlocality using an inflated quantum network with multiple copies of entangled states.

### Motivation

Existing tests of genuine multipartite nonlocality are often vulnerable to noise and require complex setups; this work aims to develop a more robust and practical approach.

### Method

The authors construct an inflated network where multiple copies of a multipartite entangled state are distributed and measured, leveraging network topology to certify nonlocality without assuming perfect devices.

### Result

The proposed method is shown to be noise-robust and can detect genuine multipartite nonlocality even in the presence of significant noise, outperforming standard Bell tests.

### Conclusion

This work provides a new tool for foundational studies and potential applications in device-independent quantum information processing, with implications for quantum networks and cryptography.

*Keywords: genuine multipartite nonlocality, quantum network, entanglement, noise-robust, Bell nonlocality*

### Motivation

To combine the advantages of fluxonium qubits (highly tunable energy levels spanning MHz to GHz) with high-coherence mechanical resonators, enabling new regimes for quantum control and sensing.

### Method

The authors theoretically design a hybrid system where a fluxonium qubit is capacitively coupled to a mechanical resonator, and analyze the coupling strength, decoherence sources, and potential for sideband cooling and state transfer.

### Result

They show that the fluxonium qubit can be tuned to match the mechanical frequency, enabling strong coupling and efficient quantum operations, with predicted coherence times sufficient for basic quantum protocols.

### Conclusion

The fluxonium-mechanical hybrid system is a promising platform for quantum transduction and sensing, with future work needed to experimentally realize the predicted coupling and mitigate noise.

### TL;DR

This paper theoretically and experimentally demonstrates bipartite entanglement generation in parametric Josephson circuits using frequency comb pumping, relevant for continuous-variable quantum computing in the microwave domain.

### Motivation

To overcome dissipation challenges in microwave systems and create high-quality cluster states for continuous-variable quantum computing, which are well-established in optics but limited in microwaves.

### Method

The authors use a parametric Josephson circuit driven by a frequency comb pump to generate two-mode squeezed states and bipartite entanglement, characterized via covariance matrix measurements and entanglement witnesses.

### Result

They observe significant bipartite entanglement between microwave frequency modes, with squeezing levels exceeding 3 dB, and demonstrate tunability of the entanglement structure via pump parameters.

### Conclusion

This work provides a scalable path toward generating large-scale cluster states in superconducting circuits, with implications for quantum computing and quantum networks in the microwave regime.

*Keywords: Josephson parametric amplifier, frequency comb, bipartite entanglement, continuous-variable quantum computing, superconducting circuits*

### TL;DR

### Motivation

Graph states are crucial for quantum information tasks but suffer from noise in NISQ devices; understanding how noise affects their entanglement is essential for practical applications.

### Method

The authors consider a noise model where initial qubits undergo depolarizing noise before CZ gate applications, and they derive a method to calculate coherent information as a lower bound on distillable entanglement.

### Result

They identify patterns on how adding nodes or edges affects bipartite distillable entanglement and find a family of graph states that maintain positive coherent information for any non-maximal depolarizing noise.

### Conclusion

## 14. Cost Of Quantum Secret Key

- **Score**: 8.5 / 10  |  **Recommended**: ✅ Yes
- **Source**: Other  |  **Feed**: Quantum
- **Direction**: Quantum Communication
- **Published**: 2026-05-06
- **Authors**: Karol Horodecki, Leonard Sikorski, Siddhartha Das, And Mark M. Wilde
- **Link**: [https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-05-06-2098/](https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-05-06-2098/)
- **Tags**: `tin`  `entanglement`  `entropy`  `quantum secret key`  `key cost`

### TL;DR

This paper develops a resource theory of quantum secret key, defining key cost and showing its relation to key of formation and relative entropy of entanglement, with implications for privacy creation-distillation irreversibility.

### Motivation

To understand the fundamental resource cost of generating quantum secret key and to establish a resource-theoretic framework for privacy, analogous to entanglement theory.

### Method

The authors define key cost and key of formation, prove that regularized key of formation upper bounds key cost via a privacy dilution protocol, and lower bound key cost by regularized relative entropy of entanglement.

### Result

Key cost is bounded above by regularized key of formation and below by regularized relative entropy of entanglement, indicating irreversibility for certain states; several entanglement measures coincide for mixed-state privacy analogues.

### Conclusion

The resource theory of quantum secret key reveals fundamental limits and irreversibility in privacy distillation, with implications for quantum cryptography and device-independent protocols.

*Keywords: quantum secret key, key cost, key of formation, privacy dilution, relative entropy of entanglement*

Relativistic effects must be considered for precise quantum metrology in non-inertial frames, and the results may inform quantum communication protocols in curved spacetime.

### Method

The authors use techniques from quantum information theory, including entropy accumulation and concentration of measure, to derive lower bounds on the number of T gates or other non-Clifford gates in doped Clifford circuits that achieve approximate unitary designs.

### TL;DR

This paper explores the large-N limit of spin squeezing in Bose-Einstein condensates, revealing a nonlinear qubit dynamics that enables quantum state discrimination and autonomous state discrimination in open systems.

### Motivation

To understand the behavior of spin squeezing in the large-N limit where two-particle entanglement is suppressed, and to exploit the resulting nonlinearity for quantum computation and state discrimination.

### Method

The authors analyze the evolution of a spin coherent state under one-axis twisting in the large-N limit, deriving a nonlinear Gross-Pitaevskii equation for the qubit dynamics. They also consider an open-system variant with dissipation.

### Result

In the large-N limit, the center of the coherent state evolves as a nonlinear qubit, enabling single-input quantum state discrimination described by a Viviani curve on the Bloch sphere. The open-system variant can generate two basins of attraction for autonomous state discrimination.

### Conclusion

Two-component Bose-Einstein condensates are a promising platform for realizing nonlinear qubits with applications in quantum state discrimination and potentially quantum computation.

*Keywords: spin squeezing, nonlinear qubit, quantum state discrimination, Bose-Einstein condensate, Gross-Pitaevskii equation*

### Motivation

### Method

Analytical derivation of DLA dimensions, centers, and decompositions for general graphs, with explicit calculations for cycle and complete graphs using representation theory and Lie algebra techniques.

### Result

For cycle graphs, the DLA decomposes into a 2-dimensional center and a semisimple component isomorphic to multiple copies of su(2), leading to a closed-form variance of the cost function and proving no barren plateaus. For complete graphs, the DLA dimension is n^2 - 1 with an explicit basis.

### Conclusion

The work provides rigorous analytical results on QAOA trainability, showing that certain graph structures avoid barren plateaus, and offers tools for further study of variational quantum algorithms.

This paper establishes foundations for bosonic quantum complexity theory, defining continuous-variable complexity classes and analyzing the hardness of bosonic Hamiltonian problems.

### Motivation

Quantum computing with continuous-variable systems (e.g., light) lacks a well-defined complexity theory, hindering understanding of their computational power and limitations.

### Method

The authors introduce bosonic generalizations of BQP, QMA, and the local Hamiltonian problem, and analyze their relationships to classical and discrete-variable quantum complexity classes using techniques from computational complexity theory.

### Result

Gaussian quantum dynamics is equivalent to BQL; higher-degree gate classes have BQP lower and EXPSPACE upper bounds; expectation values of polynomial bosonic observables are in PSPACE; bosonic Hamiltonian problems range from NP-complete to undecidable depending on the stellar rank.

### Conclusion

The work provides a complexity-theoretic framework for bosonic computations, revealing new classes and open problems, and highlighting the role of non-Gaussian resources in computational hardness.

*Keywords: bosonic quantum computing, continuous-variable complexity, Gaussian dynamics, stellar rank, bosonic Hamiltonian*

- **Tags**: `tin`  `symmetries`  `quantum cellular automata`  `symmetric subalgebra`  `anyon permutation`

### TL;DR

This paper classifies quantum cellular automata on symmetric subalgebras of one-dimensional spin systems with finite Abelian group symmetry using topological invariants.

### Motivation

To understand the structure and classification of quantum cellular automata that preserve a given symmetry, which is important for understanding topological phases and dualities.

### Method

The authors define subalgebra quantum cellular automata (QCA) on the symmetric subalgebra of a finite Abelian group symmetry, and classify them using two invariants: a homomorphism to anyon permutations in the corresponding gauge theory, and a generalized GNVW index.

### Result

They show that two subalgebra QCAs are equivalent up to finite-depth symmetric circuits iff they have the same anyon permutation and index. They also show that the Kramers-Wannier duality corresponds to a nontrivial anyon permutation and has an irrational index, preventing extension to a full QCA.

### Conclusion

The classification provides a complete understanding of symmetric QCAs in one dimension, with implications for dualities and topological phases, but the paper is primarily theoretical and not directly applicable to the user's research interests.

*Keywords: quantum cellular automata, symmetric subalgebra, anyon permutation, GNVW index, Kramers-Wannier duality*

### Motivation

To determine whether quantum neural networks can provide a computational advantage over classical methods, specifically by analyzing the neural tangent kernel.

### Method

The authors replace the average over initialization parameters in the NTK definition with an average over four discrete Clifford operations, enabling efficient classical simulation.

### Result

The NTK for a broad class of QNNs can be computed classically in polynomial time, and combined with Gaussian process equivalence, the expected output of wide trained QNNs is classically tractable.

### Conclusion

Wide quantum neural networks of the considered type cannot achieve quantum advantage, and the work provides a classical algorithm for their analysis.

They construct a nearest-neighbour Clifford circuit with a brickwork pattern and add disordered non-Clifford gates, then analyze operator spreading, fragmentation, and spectral form factor.

### TL;DR

This paper introduces quantum Margulis codes, a new class of QLDPC codes with efficient min-sum decoding and improved error floor performance compared to bivariate bicycle codes.

### Motivation

To construct QLDPC codes that can be decoded efficiently without ordered statistics decoding, addressing error degeneracy issues in quantum LDPC decoding.

### Method

Using the two-block group algebra framework to derive quantum Margulis codes from classical Margulis LDPC codes, and proposing an algorithm to control girth (minimum 6 or 8) for code construction.

### Result

Quantum Margulis codes of lengths 240 and 642 show significantly better error floor performance than bivariate bicycle codes under min-sum decoding with linear complexity.

### Conclusion

Quantum Margulis codes offer a promising alternative for fault-tolerant quantum computation with efficient decoding, though the work is limited to code capacity noise models and further study under realistic noise is needed.

They propose a logarithmic-qubit encoding that maps N physical sites to log N qubits, a compatible variational circuit, and a Gray-code-inspired measurement strategy that requires only O(log N) measurement settings.

### Result

The total space-time sampling volume in a variational loop is reduced from O(N^2) to O(log^2 N), enabling exponential reduction in hardware requirements.

### Conclusion

The approach allows simulation of large structured Hamiltonians on smaller quantum registers, extending the reach of variational quantum algorithms on near-term devices.

*Keywords: logarithmic-qubit encoding, variational quantum algorithm, Gray-code measurement, solid-state Hamiltonian, quantum simulation*

- **Link**: [https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1367-2630/ae6e99](https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1367-2630/ae6e99)
- **Tags**: `tin`  `noise`  `confinement`  `patch-potential heating`  `ion trap`

### TL;DR

This paper presents a geometry-resolved framework to evaluate patch-potential heating in ion traps, demonstrating that skeleton-electrode designs can suppress motional heating by over 50% compared to conventional blade traps.

### Motivation

Motional heating from patch potentials is a major decoherence source in trapped-ion quantum computing. The authors aimed to understand the spatial origin of heating and design electrode geometries to mitigate it.

### Method

They developed a quantitative framework to compute patch-potential noise in realistic 3D Paul traps, applied it to a skeleton-electrode architecture and a conventional blade trap, and generated patch-by-patch heating maps to identify hotspots.

### Result

The skeleton geometry suppresses heating by >50% (axial mode 0.52, radial mode 0.48 relative to blade trap). Over 99% of noise comes from electrodes within 500 μm of the ion, and axial heating has a non-monotonic profile with hotspots at ~110 μm. Reconfiguring electrode gaps to align with hotspots reduces axial heating even with increased surface area.

### Conclusion

Geometry-guided design, specifically the skeleton architecture, is a practical route to suppress heating in ion traps, and the approach is compatible with advanced fabrication like metal additive manufacturing.

*Keywords: patch-potential heating, ion trap, skeleton electrode, motional heating suppression, geometry-resolved framework*

- **Link**: [https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae5ea2](https://iopscience.iop.org/article/10.1088/1361-6633/ae5ea2)
- **Tags**: `tin`  `strain`  `compression`  `bilayer`  `microscopy`

### TL;DR

This paper studies strain relaxation in MoS2/WS2 and MoS2/WSe2 hetero-bilayers using microscopy, focusing on lattice mismatch effects in van der Waals epitaxy.

### Motivation

To understand how lattice mismatch influences strain relaxation in van der Waals heterostructures, which is critical for epitaxial growth and device quality.

### Method

Epitaxial growth of MoS2 on WS2 or WSe2 substrates, followed by annular dark-field scanning transmission electron microscopy (ADF-STEM) to image moiré patterns and analyze lattice distortions.

### Result

Small mismatch (0.22%) yields fully commensurate structure via compression; large mismatch (3.96%) leads to incommensurate moiré patterns with elongation, rotation, and bent fringes, indicating local rotational distortions as a strain release mechanism.

### Conclusion

Moiré pattern analysis is a powerful tool for visualizing strain relaxation in 2D heterostructures, enabling strain engineering for electronic and optoelectronic applications.

*Keywords: strain relaxation, van der Waals epitaxy, moiré pattern, transition metal dichalcogenides, lattice mismatch*

### TL;DR

A method called implosion carving uses swollen hydrogel and controlled shrinkage to fabricate high-refractive-index-contrast 3D nanostructures for visible-light photonics.

### Motivation

To overcome limitations in 3D nanoprinting for visible-light photonic structures, particularly achieving high refractive index contrast and nanometre resolution.

### Method

Analysis of large catalogs of radio galaxies and quasars to measure the dipole in matter distribution and compare it with the CMB dipole, including assessment of biases and systematics.

### Result

### Conclusion

The anomaly may challenge the standard ΛCDM model, warranting further investigation into systematic effects or alternative cosmological models.

- **Tags**: `dynamics`  `prediction`  `top quark pair production`  `non-relativistic QCD`  `quasi-bound states`

### TL;DR

The ATLAS Collaboration reports an 8-sigma excess in top quark pair production near threshold, consistent with quasi-bound state formation in non-relativistic QCD.

### Motivation

To test non-perturbative QCD effects near the top pair production threshold and search for evidence of quasi-bound states.

### Method

Analysis of 140 fb^{-1} of 13 TeV pp collision data, comparing baseline pQCD predictions with an extended model including NRQCD bound states via a profile-likelihood fit to angular observables.

### Result

Observed cross-section of 2.1 ± 0.4 pb, with an excess over pQCD predictions with >8 sigma significance, consistent with color-singlet, spin-singlet S-wave quasi-bound states.

### Conclusion

The results support NRQCD predictions of quasi-bound states near threshold, providing new insights into top quark dynamics and strong interaction.

*Keywords: top quark pair production, non-relativistic QCD, quasi-bound states, ATLAS, cross-section enhancement*

The mechanisms governing cardiomyocyte ploidy are poorly understood, and this study aims to uncover genetic factors that regulate ploidy and its link to cardiac structure.

### Method

Genome-wide association mapping in a rat model, combined with targeted loss-of-function experiments, to identify and validate Shroom3 as a key gene.

### Result

Shroom3 loss-of-function leads to increased polyploidy and ventricular dilation, suggesting a role in maintaining normal ploidy and cardiac morphology.

### Conclusion

Shroom3 is a critical regulator of cardiomyocyte ploidy and cardiac development, with implications for understanding congenital heart defects and cardiac regeneration.
